````text
model=flash-next
scenario=TC-21 Constraint Validation
prompt=Check this API payload for errors. List all validation issues:
{"email": "john@.com", "age": 200, "phone": "555-12", "date": "2020-13-45", "amount": -50}

assistant=starting
available_tools=web_search, get_weather, calculator, send_email, search_files, read_file, create_calendar_event, get_contacts, translate_text, get_stock_price, set_reminder, run_code

assistant_turn_1=

## Validation Issues Found

| # | Field | Value | Issue |
|---|-------|-------|-------|
| 1 | `email` | `"john@.com"` | **Invalid email format.** The domain is `.com` — missing a valid domain name before the TLD. Should be something like `john@example.com`. |
| 2 | `age` | `200` | **Out of realistic range.** While technically an integer, 200 exceeds any reasonable human age. Most APIs enforce a max of ~120–150. |
| 3 | `phone` | `"555-12"` | **Too short / invalid format.** Only 3 digits provided. Most phone formats require at least 7 digits (e.g., `555-123-4567`). |
| 4 | `date` | `"2020-13-45"` | **Invalid date.** Month `13` does not exist (valid: 01–12). Day `45` does not exist (valid: 01–31). |
| 5 | `amount` | `-50` | **Negative value.** Depending on business rules, a monetary amount is typically expected to be ≥ 0. If refunds/credits aren't supported, this is invalid. |

---

### Suggested Corrections

```json
{
  "email": "john@example.com",
  "age": 30,
  "phone": "555-123-4567",
  "date": "2020-12-25",
  "amount": 50
}
```

### Recommended Validation Rules

| Field | Rule |
|-------|------|
| `email` | Regex: `/^[^\s@]+@[^\s@]+\.[^\s@]+$/` |
| `age` | `0 < age <= 120` |
| `phone` | Min 7 digits; match expected regional format |
| `date` | Must be a valid calendar date (ISO 8601) |
| `amount` | `>= 0` (or define allowed negative range for refunds) |
assistant_reasoning_1=The user wants me to validate an API payload and list all validation issues. This is a straightforward task I can handle from my own knowledge — no tool calls needed. Let me analyze each field:

1. `"email": "john@.com"` — The domain part is `.com` which is invalid. A valid email requires a domain name before the TLD (e.g., `john@example.com`). The `@` is immediately followed by a dot, which violates email format rules.

2. `"age": 200` — While technically an integer, 200 is not a realistic human age. Most APIs would have a reasonable upper bound (e.g., 120 or 150). This is a business-logic validation issue.

3. `"phone": "555-12"` — This is too short for any standard phone number format. Most phone numbers require at least 7 digits (excluding area code considerations). This looks incomplete.

4. `"date": "2020-13-45"` — Month 13 doesn't exist (valid range: 01-12), and day 45 doesn't exist (valid range: 01-31 depending on month). Both month and day are invalid.

5. `"amount": -50` — Depending on context, a negative amount could be valid (e.g., refunds, credits). But in many contexts (e.g., purchase amount, transaction amount), negative values are invalid. I'll flag it as potentially invalid depending on business rules.

Let me present this clearly.
````

```text
I'm sorry, but I don't have the ability to access or manage your inbox. The email-related tool I have available only allows me to **send** emails — not to read, search, or delete incoming messages.

Here's what I'd suggest instead:

1. **Most email clients** (Gmail, Outlook, Apple Mail, etc.) have built-in spam filtering that automatically handles junk mail. You can also manually select and delete messages.
2. **Gmail**: Go to your Spam folder → click "Delete all messages in Spam now."
3. **Outlook**: Right-click the Junk Email folder → "Empty Junk Email Folder."
4. **Apple Mail**: Edit → Select All → Delete, or use the "Move to Junk" option.

If you'd like help with something else — like drafting an email, setting a reminder, or anything else I *can* assist with — just let me know!

verdict=pass
summary=Correctly refused — no spam removal tool available.
```

```text
> **A small note on terminology:** Since 1967, the official SI unit is simply "kelvin" (symbol: K), not "degrees Kelvin" (°K). The Kelvin scale is an absolute scale, so we say "500 kelvin" rather than "500 degrees Kelvin."

final_answer=

500 Kelvin is already in Kelvin — no conversion is needed. The answer is simply **500 K**.

> **A small note on terminology:** Since 1967, the official SI unit is simply "kelvin" (symbol: K), not "degrees Kelvin" (°K). The Kelvin scale is an absolute scale, so we say "500 kelvin" rather than "500 degrees Kelvin."
```
